    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp) while MLM collapses to vanilla level.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Regularization mechanism.}\nFrozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp), attack-dependent label sensitivity ($d \\approx 0$ on DD/FITD, $d$\\,=\\,0.44 on ActorAttack at $n$\\,=\\,10), and mean pooling matching BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) converge: classification DAPT provides robustness primarily through optimization regularization, with residual semantic contribution on structurally complex attacks, paralleling self-supervised results where training structure matters more than label content~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}. Unlike random-label memorization~\\citep{zhang2021understanding,maennel2020random}, random-label \\emph{pretraining} here produces transferable representations, suggesting that classification structure regularizes the space. Three extensions emerge: MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training despite single-LLM effectiveness, frozen probing near-parity coexists with 8\\,pp end-to-end gaps, and the hierarchy reverses on human data.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn evaluations, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders, providing robustness through optimization regularization rather than semantic learning; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness (TOST-confirmed on DD at $n$\\,=\\,5; $d$\\,=\\,0.09 on ActorAttack), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}), offering a practical alternative given matched benign distributions (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll multi-turn findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B (350 training, 76--118 test per family). ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. Whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question (length confound analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only: benign data is IID, so OOD F1 figures are upper bounds (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate the capacity effect, and whether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open. Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. Total pipeline cost is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

    "originalFile": "% abstract.tex — D048 deep writing optimization\n\\begin{abstract}\nDetecting unseen multi-turn jailbreaks requires domain adaptation, but the form of adaptation matters.\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks spread adversarial intent across benign-seeming turns, and existing defenses fail to generalize across attack families: Llama Guard~3, a widely deployed turn-level safety classifier, detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation.\nWe isolate the factors behind cross-attack generalization on synthetic multi-turn data through controlled ablation: thirteen encoder configurations at three scales (125 to 355M parameters) decompose the contributions of domain exposure, classification supervision, label quality, and granularity on three out-of-distribution (OOD) attack families and two training compositions.\nOn single-source synthetic data, continued pretraining closes roughly 60 percentage points of the OOD gap without labels. This effectiveness is condition-dependent: on mixed-LLM training data, unsupervised pretraining collapses while classification-based adaptation remains the strongest variant (DD OOD F1\\,=\\,0.818 vs.\\ 0.291 without adaptation).\nAcross three architectures (125--355M), the set of attacks requiring adaptation shrinks with scale, though architecture and pretraining differences prevent isolating scale as the sole factor.\nClassification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic evaluations (observed across 3 seeds), with limited sensitivity to label correctness, but this benefit reverses on human-authored data.\nThese findings suggest classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization, not semantic feature learning.\n\\end{abstract}\n",

    "newString": "scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels on template-based attacks (TOST-confirmed on DD and FITD at $n$\\,=\\,10) but show moderate sensitivity on structurally complex attacks ($d$\\,=\\,0.44 on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant ($\\Delta < 1$\\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.",
    "originalFile": "% introduction.tex — expanded for 8-page target\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across individually benign turns. Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} all rely on persuasion as their core mechanism: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} identify strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Because individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, no single-turn filter can distinguish them from legitimate conversation.\n\nExisting defenses leave a critical gap. Production classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and miss multi-turn manipulation; Llama Guard~3 detects only 1.8\\% of multi-turn jailbreaks in our evaluation (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). Multi-turn defenses (DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026}, BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid}, G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025}) model conversation-level structure but each evaluates on a single attack family. None answers the central question: \\emph{what representation properties enable a detector to generalize across attack families it has never seen?}\n\nPretrained language understanding does not suffice at base model scale: vanilla DeBERTa-v3~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation (cf.\\ DeepContext) drops from F1\\,=\\,1.000 in-distribution to 0.312 on OOD Deceptive Delight, and GPT-5.5 reaches only 0.191--0.449 F1 with 30--47\\% unparseable responses (Appendix~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). The bottleneck is distributional: cross-attack generalization requires domain adaptation, not a better classifier.\n\nOur pipeline, \\textbf{DisPeD} (Disentangled Persuasion Detection), separates encoder adaptation (Stage~1) from temporal classification (Stage~2, a frozen BiGRU; Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). Seven Stage~1 variants systematically vary training objectives while sharing the same two-stage pipeline (Section~\\ref{sec:method}); the contribution is the decomposition, not the pipeline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_architecture.pdf}\n\\caption{DisPeD architecture. Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa encoder on jailbreak conversation text (seven variant objectives). Stage~2 aggregates frozen \\texttt{[CLS]} embeddings via BiGRU for conversation-level jailbreak detection. Only user turns are processed.}\n\\label{fig:architecture}\n\\end{figure}\n\nControlled ablation across DeBERTa-v3 and RoBERTa~\\citep{liu2019roberta} on four attack families and two training compositions shows that for base-size encoders, classification-based adaptation is the most robust variant on synthetic multi-turn OOD attacks. MLM collapses on mixed-LLM training (F1\\,=\\,0.289 $\\approx$ vanilla) while classification DAPT maintains 0.818; RoBERTa-large (355M) handles ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} without adaptation. Classification supervision is associated with substantially lower prediction variance on synthetic data but not on human-authored conversations (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).\n\nOur contributions:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition identifying the most robust adaptation and three supplementary negative results.} Controlled ablation across thirteen encoder configurations on synthetic multi-turn data shows classification-based DAPT is the most robust variant; unsupervised pretraining is effective on single-source data but collapses on mixed-LLM training. We report three supplementary findings that challenge common assumptions: scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD at $n$\\,=\\,5; $d$\\,=\\,0.09 on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant ($\\Delta < 1$\\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Architecture- and scale-dependent observation on domain adaptation.} Three encoder scales (125M, 184M, 355M) suggest adaptation need shrinks with model size, though encoders differ in architecture; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate scale. RoBERTa-large handles structurally complex attacks without adaptation (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_encoder}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Mechanistic interpretation: optimization regularization, not semantic learning.} Three null results converge: frozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp), label content irrelevance ($d$\\,=\\,0.09), and temporal modeling irrelevance ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) are consistent with classification supervision providing robustness through optimization regularization that stabilizes downstream training. This benefit is condition-dependent (variance reversal on human data) but classification DAPT remains strongest under mixed-LLM training (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). Continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:data_efficiency}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

We report three supplementary findings that challenge common assumptions: scrambled labels perform comparably to correct labels (TOST-confirmed on DD at $n$\\,=\\,5; $d$\\,=\\,0.09 on ActorAttack), temporal order is irrelevant ($\\Delta < 1$\\,pp), and label granularity does not affect detection.",

    "originalFile": "% conclusion.tex — compressed for 8-page limit\n\n\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Topic shortcut and label equivalence.}\nThree observations argue against a topic shortcut: unstable topic OOD (F1\\,=\\,0.541$\\pm$0.207), scrambled labels within 3.9\\,pp, and label-free MLM detection (F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.97).\n\n\\paragraph{Single-LLM confound.}\nToxicChat (+18--42\\,pp; Table~\\ref{tab:human_data}) and cross-LLM experiments (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_llm}) confirm transfer. Mixed-LLM 9-class maintains DD F1\\,=\\,0.818$\\pm$0.096 (+53\\,pp) while MLM collapses to vanilla level.\n\n\\paragraph{Variance reversal on human data.}\nClassification supervision is associated with lower variance on synthetic OOD (4--20$\\times$ observed range, 3 seeds); on ToxicChat this reverses (std\\,=\\,0.087 vs.\\ 0.013). Frozen probing localizes MLM instability to Stage-2 (Appendix~\\ref{app:frozen_verify}).\n\n\\paragraph{Encoder capacity and lexical analysis.}\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed. TF-IDF collapses on ActorAttack (0.261 vs.\\ 0.961 on DD).\n\n\\paragraph{Regularization mechanism.}\nFrozen probing near-parity (AA gap 3.1\\,pp), label irrelevance ($d$\\,=\\,0.09), and mean pooling matching BiGRU ($|\\Delta| < 1$\\,pp) converge: classification DAPT provides robustness through optimization regularization, not semantic feature learning, paralleling self-supervised results where training structure matters more than label content~\\citep{chen2020simclr,grill2020byol}. Unlike random-label memorization~\\citep{zhang2021understanding,maennel2020random}, random-label \\emph{pretraining} here produces transferable representations, suggesting that classification structure regularizes the space. Three extensions emerge: MLM collapses under mixed-LLM training despite single-LLM effectiveness, frozen probing near-parity coexists with 8\\,pp end-to-end gaps, and the hierarchy reverses on human data.\n\n\\paragraph{Benign distribution as the remaining bottleneck.}\nOOD F1 figures are upper bounds (benign is IID). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); cross-LLM error decomposition confirms collapse is driven by false positives on unfamiliar benign text.\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\nOn synthetic multi-turn evaluations, classification-based DAPT is the most robust adaptation for base-size encoders, providing robustness through optimization regularization rather than semantic learning; unsupervised pretraining collapses under mixed-LLM training. Negative results on label correctness (TOST-confirmed on DD at $n$\\,=\\,5; $d$\\,=\\,0.09 on ActorAttack), temporal order, and domain specificity constrain which properties matter; continued pretraining is 266$\\times$ faster than LLM-as-judge (Appendix~\\ref{app:efficiency}), offering a practical alternative given matched benign distributions (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}).\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nAll multi-turn findings derive from synthetic conversations generated by Qwen3-8B (350 training, 76--118 test per family). ToxicChat validates transfer to human-authored single-turn attacks (+18--42\\,pp over vanilla), but no human-generated multi-turn jailbreak dataset exists. Whether the evidence hierarchy generalizes to human-authored multi-turn attacks remains the primary open question (length confound analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:length_independence}).\n\nOOD results reflect attack-side generalization only: benign data is IID, so OOD F1 figures are upper bounds (Section~\\ref{sec:discussion}). On WildChat, 9-class FPR reaches 68.1\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:wildchat_fpr}); residual calibration error (ECE\\,=\\,0.126--0.141; Appendix~\\ref{app:calibration}) suggests temperature scaling is needed for deployment.\n\nThree encoders (125M--355M) differ in architecture and pretraining; same-architecture scaling is needed to isolate the capacity effect, and whether findings hold at LLM-scale encoders (7B+) remains open. Only persuasion-mediated multi-turn attacks fall within scope; single-turn token-level attacks~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, non-persuasive strategies, and non-text modalities are not addressed. Total pipeline cost is under 4 GPU-hours on a single A100.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work is defensive: it improves detection of multi-turn jailbreak attacks rather than developing new attacks. We acknowledge dual-use risk, as the persuasion taxonomy could inform adversarial actors. We mitigate this by using only synthetic conversations (no real harmful content), withholding attack-generation prompts, and focusing contributions on detection mechanisms. No human subjects were involved; all data will be released under CC BY-NC 4.0.\n",

Label granularity (binary vs.\\ 9-class) is a non-factor across all families.",
          " Classification supervision is associated with 4--20$\\times$ lower cross-seed variance over MLM on synthetic OOD (std 0.005 vs.\\ 0.115 on DD; observed range across 3 seeds; formal variance ratio tests are not applicable at $n$=3; cross-seed ensemble yields marginal additional gains, Appendix~\\ref{app:ensemble}). This stability benefit does not transfer to human-authored data, where classification-supervised variants exhibit the highest variance and pure MLM the lowest (Section~\\ref{sec:human_data}).",
